# Second hand check of 1,000 credited paragraphs, 2 November 2026

This is the second hand check. It measures the same two things as the first ([hand-check-2026-11-02.md](hand-check-2026-11-02.md)): how often the site credits a paragraph to the wrong person, and how often the words differ from the Official Report. It drew a fresh 1,000 speech rows with a speaker id at random from the production database on 2 November 2026, after write #11. One AI reader, Claude Sonnet, judged every row's credit against the stored copy of the Official Report, and a script compared every row's words with it. 12 rows were flagged. Royce gave 3 verdicts himself, in his inbox. The other 9 differ from the record only in kinds he had already ruled on in the first check, and an agent applied those rulings. No person read the other 988 rows.

The rules are in [hand-check-rulebook.md](../hand-check-rulebook.md), and the rule for role labels such as "The Chairman" is [ADR 0015](../adr/0015-hand-check-role-credits.md). The site's [ADR 0009](https://github.com/roycehoe/hansard/blob/master/docs/adr/0009-ai-readers-person-judges-flags.md) records the decision that AI readers read every row and a person judges the flags. The draw record and the sealed plant key's hash are in [hand-check-2/](hand-check-2/).

## Result

| Claim | Rows in 1,000 | Ceiling (one-sided exact, 95%) |
|---|---|---|
| Credited wrongly | 2 (2 Tier A) | 0.63% |
| Substantive text error | 0 | 0.30% |
| Cosmetic text error | 6 (Tier C) | 1.18% |
| Any error | 8 | 1.44% |

Each ceiling holds at 95% on its own, not jointly. "Any error" adds the 6 cosmetic text errors to the wrong credits; no row has both. The shares are counts out of 1,000, not weighted.[^weights]

One verdict is open. On row 625 (speech 1305174) the packet showed the reader and Royce the wrong source paragraph, so the credit Royce ruled wrong is right on the record (see "Row 625: a question for Royce"). His verdict stands until he rules again. If he rules the row right, the check has 1 wrong credit (ceiling 0.47%) and 7 errors of any kind (ceiling 1.31%).

The sample holds no row whose source could not be found and no row whose credit the record cannot settle after Royce's verdicts, so neither adds to the wrong count.

[^weights]: Weighted by each group's share of the frame, the wrong-credit share is 0.20% and the any-error share 0.80%. The five groups' weights differ by about 0.32%, so weighting moves neither share by more than 0.01 points.

## How sure the reader's catch rate is

Before the reader started, 30 errors were planted among the packets: 15 rows credited to the wrong member, and 15 captions, Resolved lines or other record lines credited to the member on the row above. The reader caught all 30. That does not prove it catches every error. It means that, at 95% confidence, it catches at least 90.50% of errors of those two kinds (one-sided exact: a reader that caught only 90.50% would miss none of 30 just 5% of the time).

What this means for errors the reader may have missed:

- Wrong credits. If the reader catches only 90.50%, the 2 it found stand for about 2.2 in expectation, and the wrong-credit ceiling of 0.63% becomes about 0.69%. That figure stacks two 95% bounds, so it holds at a little under 95%.
- Text errors. The reader's catch rate does not bear on them. The script compares every word of every row with the record, so a text error cannot slip past a reader.
- Other kinds of error. The plants test two kinds only. A kind that looks like neither, such as a label that names the wrong office holder, is caught at a rate this check does not measure. The chair-label check below is a second look at one such kind.

## Both checks together

The first check drew its 1,000 rows on 1 November, after write #10, and this one on 2 November, after write #11. Write #11 stored the fixes for 4 of the first check's errors, so the two samples come from slightly different databases. Taken together as one sample of 2,000 rows:

| Claim | Rows in 2,000 | Ceiling (one-sided exact, 95%) |
|---|---|---|
| Credited wrongly | 6 (first check 4, this check 2) | 0.59% |
| Substantive text error | 0 | 0.15% |
| Any error | 15 (first check 7, this check 8) | 1.15% |

The first check's figures count its row 157 as right, as Royce re-ruled it.

### Row 625: a question for Royce

Report 12665 (8 May 2019, the Protection from Online Falsehoods and Manipulation Bill) prints "Yes, please." twice. The first, at the row's place, is printed `<strong>Mr Speaker</strong>: Yes, please. [A slide was shown to hon Members.]`, the Speaker's answer to a member who asked to show a slide. The second, about 600 rows later, is printed `<strong>Mr Deputy Speaker</strong>: Yes, please.`, the Deputy Speaker's answer to Mr Saktiandi Supaat, who asked to speak in Malay. The stored row is the first: its label is "Mr Speaker", it is credited to Tan Chuan-Jin, and the row after it is the slide note. The second is stored as its own row (speech 1305789), labelled "Mr Deputy Speaker" and credited to Charles Chong. A fresh parse gives both rows as stored.

The packet builder matched the row's text to the second "Yes, please.", because the first paragraph also holds the slide note, which the parser cuts into its own row, so only the second was an exact match. The packet therefore showed the Deputy Speaker's line, and the reader and Royce judged the row against it. On the record the credit is right. This is the same kind of builder fault as the first check's row 157. Refine loop 2 fixes the builder (follow-up 2). Royce's verdict stands in every figure above until he rules on it.

## Who gave each verdict

The reader's speaker verdicts on the 1,000 rows: 998 right, 1 wrong and 1 "record doesn't say". The script found a difference that no ruling had taught in 10 rows, none of them among the reader's 2. That makes 12 flags.

No review page was published. Royce gave 3 verdicts in his inbox on 2 November 2026, and they are the record:

- Row 19 (speech 709801): credited wrongly, Tier A.
- Row 625 (speech 1305174): credited wrongly, Tier A. See the question above.
- Row 473 (speech 380638): credit right, text a cosmetic error, Tier C. The flag came from a packet builder fault (follow-up 3).

The other 9 were text flags whose reader verdict was right, and each fits a kind Royce ruled on in the first check. An agent applied those rulings and wrote them as `claude-cluster-ruling`:

1. A question number left out of the row is a listed change (data-loss register line 87): 2 rows. In one the number is printed with the label and no colon, as an asked question prints it; in the other the source paragraph runs three written questions together, and the first two sit on the rows before.
2. A speaker label left in the source paragraph, or text that sits on the neighbouring row, matches: 2 rows. In one the printed label differs from the stored one by "The" and a space; in the other "[Laughter]" is cut from inside the paragraph into the next row, which has no speaker.
3. An extra space beside italic markers is a cosmetic error, Tier C: 5 rows. For each, a check confirmed that the only change is added spaces, and that each added space touches an italic marker in the raw transcript.

The marks are in the gitignored file `plans/hand-check/main-2/marks.json`, in the first check's shape: each field records its writer, Royce's viewer id or `claude-cluster-ruling`, and how it was given, `INBOX` or `CLUSTER_RULING`.

### The chair-label check

Planted errors test wrong members and record lines, not role labels, and section 10's chair rule changed after the first check. So after the reader finished, a script listed every row whose label holds "Chairman" or names a Deputy Speaker without naming the person: 15 rows (9 "The Chairman", 3 bare "Mr Deputy Speaker", 3 Deputy Speaker labels settled by Mr or Mdm). For each it gave a fresh Claude Opus checker the packet and every chair, suspension, Speaker, Deputy Speaker and Chairman line of the whole sitting, in sitting order. The checker found all 15 right, as the reader had, so none went to Royce. It also found that the packet's role section reads marks after the row from the row's own report only, and that the builder does not read "went to the Chair" as a change of chair (follow-up 5). Neither changes a verdict.

## Side figures

These count Tier C faults that send no row to Royce.

- Rows whose stored text holds a `**` or `__` that the matched source paragraph does not: 0 of 1,000 (user ruling U6).
- Rows holding a single `_`: 14 of 1,000, all in matched pairs, which the site shows as italics.
- The gap check found 27 of 1,000 rows not followed by the next source paragraph. It is information only, not a flag.
- Taught differences, which add no flag: a question number in 34 rows, a language note in 4, and a chamber event cut into the next row in 4.

## Method

The method is the first check's, with four changes: one reader instead of two, a text script taught the kinds Royce ruled on, a chair-label check after the reader, and fresh seeds.

**Frame and draw.** The frame is every speech row with a speaker id, 1,001,902 rows, sorted as in the first check. The seeds were committed before anything ran (`c97d6ca`): 20261111 for the draw, 20261112 and 20261113 for the plant rounds and 20261114 for the live SPRS check. No earlier check used them. The draw read the frame in one read-only, repeatable-read transaction at 14:14 SGT on 2 November 2026. The frame hash is `f42cf3cce4c5c4fbf83ec7b7cb0336be578987550898ecfa59ba1c1b4f2d8140`, and an order check over the full frame passed just before the draw. The five groups (Parliaments 0 to 11, 12, 13, 14, 15) were allocated 515, 83, 133, 207 and 62 rows by largest remainder and drawn by systematic sampling from a seeded start. The 1,000 rows sit in 1,000 different reports. No row is also in the first check's sample; 340 reports are. `hand-check-2/draw.tsv` lists each row, and `hand-check-2/draw-summary.txt` the group counts, starts and weight spread.

**Database date.** The draw read production after write #11, which committed at 14:01:29 SGT on 2 November 2026 and stored the fixes for 4 of the first check's errors. The 65 reports held since write #9 stay in the frame; 11 sampled rows sit in them.

**Source.** Every row is compared with the stored copy of the Official Report (`report.content`). For 50 sampled reports chosen with the committed seed, the build also fetched today's SPRS text: the stored copy matched live SPRS in 50 of 50.

**Reader.** The 1,000 rows and 30 planted errors became 1,030 packets with random ids, in 96 shuffled batches of at most 50 packets or 80,000 characters (two batches hold one longer packet each). Each packet shows the row, the source paragraph with the lines up to the nearest speaker label above it, and, for a role label, the office-holder table entries and the sitting's chair marks. Each batch went to one Claude Sonnet agent with rulebook section 10 as the prompt, word for word. No batch was rerun. The first check had a second reader, Claude Opus; both agreed on all 1,000 rows and both caught all 30 plants, so this check kept the cheaper one (`bd2a289`).

**Planted errors.** As in the first check: 15 wrong members and 15 credited record lines. The key's SHA-256 (`hand-check-2/plant-key-round-1.sha256`) was committed before the packets were built (`55d281d`), and the key stayed sealed until the reader finished. The reader caught all 30, so the second plant round did not run.

**Text script.** Both texts are normalised as in the first check. A row matches exactly when its text equals one or more consecutive whole source paragraphs: 949 did, 51 did not, and every row's source was found. The script now marks a difference of a kind Royce ruled on (a question number, a language note, the row's own label, a chamber event cut into the next row with no speaker) and does not flag it (`64e7a43`). Replayed on the first check's packets, the taught script flags 12 rows instead of 60, and every one of the 48 it drops is a row Royce ruled not an error.

**Flags.** A row went to Royce when the reader's speaker verdict was not "right", the script found a difference of no taught kind, or the match was not exact and not explained by taught kinds alone. A row from a live-checked report whose stored copy differed would also have gone; none did. Flags that fit a kind Royce had ruled on were settled by that ruling, except that every speaker verdict other than "right" went to him.

**Role labels.** 56 sampled rows carry a role label: 33 Speaker, 5 Prime Minister, 3 Chief Minister, 3 Deputy Speaker named by Mr or Mdm, and 12 chair labels. The office-holder table settled 44; 8 entries were added for this sample, each cited to an outside source (`b6bda0f`). The sitting's own chair marks settled the 12 chair labels. The check cannot see a change of chair that the record does not print.

## Live site check

On 2 November 2026 the 12 flagged rows' reports were fetched once each from the site's API, at least 2 seconds apart. All 12 answered 200, and in each the row at the snapshot's ordinal had the snapshot's label and text. The other 988 rows were not fetched: write #12 was being applied to production while this report was written, so a fetch then would not show the database the check drew from.

Each site link in the table opens the row on its report page, built as the site's own "Copy link" button builds it: the report's slug, the row's ordinal and the first 8 words of its text, from the site's `get_speech_anchor`. For the 12 fetched rows these equal the API's own slug and anchor. Write #12 rewrites some held reports, so a link into one of them may open the report with the note that the paragraph is no longer there.

## Follow-ups

None of these was built when this report was written. Refine loop 2 takes up follow-ups 1 to 3.

1. **"Certificate of Urgency handed in."** Row 19. The clerk's record that a minister handed in a certificate, printed as its own paragraph after his words, is credited to him. The parser already gives "Certificate of Urgency signed by the President" no speaker (`_PROCEDURAL_RECORD_LINE_RE`, `services/speech.py`). Refine loop 2 adds this line and others of its family.
2. **A repeated short reply matched to the wrong paragraph.** Row 625. When a row's text appears twice in a report and only the other copy matches exactly, the packet builder takes that copy, though it sits under another label and is not followed by the row's next row. The builder should prefer a paragraph under the row's own label. The site's row is right.
3. **Source run too wide.** Row 473. Two vernacular pointers in report 22117 open with the same words, "[For vernacular speech, please refer to Appendix A". The first, in Mr Hawazi Daipi's turn, matched the row's first eight words; the row's own, in Dr Lam Pin Min's turn, prints "Appendix A*." without the space. So the packet's source run started about 5,500 words early, in another member's turn. The builder should not let a run cross into another turn when a shorter probe finds one inside the row's turn.
4. **Extra space beside italic markers.** 5 settled rows and row 473: all 6 of this check's cosmetic errors, and 2 of the first check's 3. This is now the most common error the checks find. It is Tier C and changes no word. The first check's follow-up 4 is still open.
5. **Chair marks after the row.** The packet's role section reads marks after the row from the row's own report only, and the builder does not read "Mr N. Govindasamy went to the Chair and sat down" as a change of chair (`_CHAIR_EVENT_RE`). No verdict depended on either.
